Power set operads
Mathieu Vallée

Abstract
. We introduce a systematic method for constructing set-theoretic operads via iterated application of the power set functor, and use it to uncover a hierarchy connecting several classical operads. Starting from the permutative operad, the first iteration recovers the commutative triassociative operad. The second iteration produces the substitution operad and the composition operad on simplicial complexes, two structures introduced by Ayzenberg and Abramyan–Panov in the theory of polyhedral products; we prove that both are infinitely generated. This hierarchy yields a conceptual explanation for the multiplicity of polyhedral product constructions: the arrows of any cocontinuous cocomplete symmetric monoidal category carry natural algebra structures over both operads, recovering the Cartesian, smash, and join polyhedral products as instances for different monoidal structures on topological spaces. Going further, we construct a new operad on relative simplicial complexes, governed by the join polyhedral product, which contains both the composition and the substitution operads as suboperads. As an application, pairs of piecewise-linear balls without interior vertices with their boundary spheres form a suboperad, extending the stability of the J J -construction on piecewise-linear spheres.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary:
18M70, 55U10.
Secondary:
18M05, 57Q05, 55P99, 06A07.

Introduction

Our motivation lies at the intersection of set-theoretic operads and
the theory of polyhedral products. This article pursues two related
goals: (i) to introduce a systematic construction of set-theoretic
operads via the power set functor, and (ii) to make explicit the
operadic structure underlying polyhedral products, extending
observations that have appeared implicitly or explicitly in the
literature
[
5
,
7
,
49
,
1
,
23
]
.

Set operads

Operads encode collections of abstract operations that can be composed
and that act on categories of algebras. Introduced in the 1970s by
May
[
41
]
and Boardman–Vogt
[
9
]
to
describe the structure of iterated loop spaces, they have since become
a central organizational tool across algebra, topology, and
combinatorics.

Set-theoretic
(symmetric) operads are those whose compositions
take place in the monoidal category of sets equipped with the Cartesian
product; this is the framework we work in throughout. Classical
examples include the commutative, associative, and permutative
operads
[
14
]
, as well as the commutative triassociative
operad
[
48
]
(the non-symmetric version
\operatorname{\mathit{Trias}}

was first considered by Loday–Ronco
[
34
]
). These
operads, their linearized forms, and their Koszul duals — such as the
(Pre-)Lie operad — were originally motivated by algebraic topology
and K-theory, notably through the work of
Loday
[
36
,
37
]
.
Some set operads can be generated from a monoid
[
24
]
;
the permutative operad, which plays a central role here, is one such
example.
The commutative triassociative operad have been shown to govern the combinatorics of cooperative games
[
42
]
.

In combinatorics more broadly, the operadic framework provides the
right language for studying compositions of combinatorial objects, with
applications ranging from random matrices
[
40
]
and
graph operads
[
4
]
to
mathematical physics, where for instance the notion of a Feynman category due to Kaufmann and Ward
[
31
]
encodes the combinatorics of Feynman diagrams. The Gröbner basis theory for operads in
Feynman categories was developed in
[
19
]
, whose underlying
combinatorial objects are geometric lattices, a notion closely related to matroid theory. A concrete instance is
[
20
]
,
which treats the case of boolean lattices and their building sets and
provides an operadic structure on associated toric varieties.

A natural and, until now, largely unexplored source of set operads
arises from the theory of polyhedral products.

Polyhedral products and simplicial complex operads

A
polyhedral product

[
6
]

is a topological space built from a simplicial complex
K
on the
vertex set
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
and a sequence of pairs of topological spaces

(\underline{X},\underline{Y})\coloneqq((X_{i},Y_{i}))_{i\in[n]}
, that is
Y_{i}\hookrightarrow X_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
, via

\cal Z(K;(\underline{X},\underline{Y}))\coloneqq\bigcup_{I\in K}\cal z(I;(\underline{X},\underline{Y})),\qquad\cal z(I;(\underline{X},\underline{Y}))\coloneqq\prod_{i\in[n]}Z_{i}^{I},\qquad Z_{i}^{I}\coloneqq\begin{cases}X_{i}&i\in I,\\
Y_{i}&i\notin I,\end{cases}

where the ambient monoidal structure is the Cartesian product of
topological spaces. Natural variants arise from other monoidal
structures, such as the smash product
[
6
]

or the join
[
5
]
. The central example is the

moment-angle complex
, introduced in toric
topology
[
10
,
11
]
as the
polyhedral product for the pair
(D^{2},S^{1})
; the standard reference
is
[
12
]
. Polyhedral products with all pairs
equal to
(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{\ast})
, or
(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{\ast})
, recover complements of coordinate subspace arrangements
[
12
, Section 4.7]
.
Recently, the motivic homotopy theory version of polyhedral product appeared in
[
28
]
.
Other cryptomorphic constructions have appeared such as the
dual polyhedral product

[
47
]
, polyhedral product over finite posets
[
32
]
, or the
polyhedral coproduct

[
46
]
.

Two operads on simplicial complexes play a central role in this theory.
The
substitution operad
— defined on transversal families by
Billera
[
8
]
and later reformulated for simplicial
complexes — and the
composition operad
, first described by
Erokhovets for building sets (see
[
12
, Construction 1.5.19]
)
and studied in the polyhedral product context by Ayzenberg
[
5
]
,
have proved to be powerful organizing principles.

The composition operad underlies the topological construction of
infinite families of toric manifolds via the
J
-construction

and
canonical extension
on characteristic
maps
[
7
]
, later generalized
in
[
17
,
18
]
for the classification of
toric manifolds of fixed Picard number — carried out for Picard
number
4
in
[
15
,
16
]
.
We show here that the
J
-construction is a right action of a suboperad
of the composition operad on the collection of simplicial complexes,
and we extend its stability on
piecewise-linear spheres
— the
simplicial complexes underlying (topological) toric
manifolds
[
30
]
— by considering an operad whose operations are pairs formed by a PL ball and its boundary PL sphere.

The substitution operad, on the other hand, governs higher Whitehead
products for polyhedral products
[
1
,
26
]
.
In
[
5
]
, Ayzenberg moreover showed that both operadic
compositions arise as polyhedral join products, and subsequent work
studied the cohomology
[
49
]

and homotopy type
[
23
]
of polyhedral products built
from such simplicial complexes. In this article, we modify the join
polyhedral product construction to produce a new operad by passing to
relative
simplicial complexes.

Motivated by the operadic calculus — Koszul duality, operadic
Gröbner bases
[
21
,
22
]

— we aim to study these operads and their algebras systematically.
Our key observation is that both the substitution and composition
operads on simplicial complexes arise as the second iteration of the
power set functor applied to the permutative operad, placing them
in a natural hierarchy generated by a single construction.

Contributions

The contributions of this article are organized around four themes.

The power set operad functor.

Our central observation is that the (reduced) power set functor
\overline{\wp}

and its variants are lax monoidal endofunctors of the monoidal category
(\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathsf{Coll},\circ,\mathsf{I})
of
\mathbb{S}
-collections equipped with the composition product. By a classical result on monoidal monads
[
33
,
44
]
, they lift to endofunctors — and in fact forms a monad — on the category
\mathsf{Op}(\mathsf{Set})
of set-theoretic operads (
Definition

2.4
,
Proposition

2.3
,
Corollary

2.10
).

Applied to the permutative operad, the first iteration recovers the commutative triassociative operad:

\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})\cong\operatorname{\mathit{ComTrias}}.

Simplicial complex operads.

The second iteration
\overline{\wp}^{2}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
and its variants produce operads on simplicial complexes that we identify with the substitution operad
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
and the composition operad
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
of
[
1
,
5
]
.
We prove that all of these — including their unreduced and non-empty
variants — have
infinitely many generators

(
Theorem

3.21
). We describe suboperads isomorphic to

\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
and provide some of the left and right modules of each, expressed in terms of classical operations on simplicial complexes: ghost vertices, disjoint unions, duplicate vertices, wedge operations, joins, and the
J
-construction (
Remark

3.13
).

Algebras and polyhedral products.

The arrows of any small cocomplete cocontinuous symmetric monoidal
category
(\mathrm{C},\otimes,e)
carry algebra structures
over both
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}\})
and

(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})

(
Theorem

3.24
,
Theorem

3.25
), with the

polyhedral product maps

\overrightarrow{\cal Z}_{n}
and

\overrightarrow{\cal Z}_{n}^{c}
as structure maps. This operadic
framework simultaneously accounts for the polyhedral product,
the polyhedral smash product, the polyhedral join product, and the
simplicial join product as instances of the same construction for
different monoidal structures (
Table

2
).
Dual statements for small complete continuous categories yield polyhedral
limit maps
\overrightarrow{\cal L}_{n}
and
\overrightarrow{\cal L}_{n}^{c}

(
Theorem

3.29
).

The simplicial join operad and relative simplicial complexes.

Relative simplicial complexes were introduced by Stanley
[
45
]
and have recently come to the fore via relative Stanley–Reisner theory
[
2
]
, playing a central role in Adiprasito’s proof of the
g
-conjecture for simplicial spheres
[
3
]
.
We further introduce the
simplicial join operad

(\mathsf{relscpx},\{\circ_{k}^{\ast}\})
on relative simplicial complexes, whose composition is built upon the simplicial join product.
It contains the substitution and composition operads as suboperads (
Proposition

4.14
), and the arrows of
(\mathrm{C},\otimes,e)
carry a natural algebra structure over it, with the join polyhedral product map
\overrightarrow{\cal M}_{n}
as structure map (
Theorem

4.22
).
As an application, neat PL pairs
(B,\partial B)
— classical objects of PL topology
[
29
,
43
]
consisting of PL balls without interior vertices paired with their boundary spheres — form a suboperad of
(\mathsf{relscpx},\{\circ_{k}^{\ast}\})
(
Proposition

4.19
), extending the stability of the
J
-construction on PL spheres and providing a new operadic framework for PL topology.

Structure of the article

We start by
Section

1
that recalls all definitions, or point to references, that are used in this article.

Section

2
develops the two main characters of this article: the power set functor and its reduced version. In particular, we give its first properties in
Section

2.1
, then apply once the reduced power set functor to classical set operad in
Section

2.2
, and to the unreduced case in
Section

2.3
. The case of the permutative operad is computed independently in
Section

2.4
, as it is of major importance in this article, as well as algebras over its powers.

In
Section

3
we look at power two operads obtained by the permutative operad and recover operads on simplicial complexes: the substitution operad in
Section

3.1
and in
Section

3.2
, and the composition operad in
Section

3.3
. We then compute some of their suboperads in
Section

3.4
and prove that both are infinitely generated in
Section

3.5
. We end this section with
Section

3.6
by constructing algebras over these two operads, which one example is the polyhedral product construction.

Section

4
is devoted to introducing the simplicial join operad on relative simplicial complexes, which is based upon the simplicial join product, whose definitions are recalled in
Section

4.1
. The definition and some properties of the simplicial join operad are given in
Section

4.2
. A suboperad focusing on piecewise-linear topology is described in
Section

4.3
. Finally, we describe algebras over the simplicial join operad in
Section

4.4
.

Acknowledgements

I am grateful to my advisor Bruno Vallette for introducing me to operad theory, for his constant support and invaluable comments, and for many fruitful discussions on the content of this paper.
I also thank Anthony Bahri for motivating conversations about polyhedral products, which guided me toward the writing of this article.

1.
Recollections

For the sake of self-completeness and to accommodate the different communities addressed by this article, we recall here some notions from set, category, and operad theory, and also fix the relevant notation.

1.1.
Set theoretical notions

For a set
S
, the
power set of
S
is the set of subsets of
S
and is denoted by
\wp(S)
.
It describes a functor
\wp
in the category of sets, denoted by
\mathsf{Set}
.
We also define the
reduced power set
functor
\overline{\wp}\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
by
\overline{\wp}(S)\coloneqq\wp(S)\setminus\{\varnothing\}
.
Note that
\wp(\varnothing)=\{\varnothing\}
and
\overline{\wp}(\varnothing)=\varnothing
.
For
X\in\wp(S)
we denote by
\complement X\coloneqq S\setminus X
the
complementary
of
X
in
S
.
Note that for a set
S
,
\complement\colon\wp(S)\to\wp(S)
is an involution.

When
S\in\mathsf{Set}
is finite, we have
|\wp(S)|=2^{|S|}
and
|\overline{\wp}(S)|=2^{|S|}-1
.
By convention,
\wp^{0}(S)\coloneqq S
.
A set is
based on

S
if it is an element of
\wp^{k}(S)
, for some
k\geq 0
, called its
power
.
We define a scale of complement operations for sets based on
S
with any power
k
as follows.

Definition 1.1

(Derivative of complement operations)
.

Let
S
be a set and
k\geq 1
. For
1\leq\ell\leq k
, we define recursively the involution
\complement^{(\ell)}\colon\wp^{k}(S)\to\wp^{k}(S)
by setting:

•

\complement^{(1)}\coloneqq\complement
, and

•

\complement^{(\ell)}(K)\coloneqq\left\{\complement^{(\ell-1)}I\mid I\in K\right\}
, when
\ell>1
.

For convenience, we will write
\complement^{\prime}\coloneqq\complement^{(2)}
.

The
disjoint union
of two sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
with empty intersection is denoted by
S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}
.

1.2.
Families of subsets

A
family of subsets
on
S
is a set based on
S
with power
2
.
We now recall some well-known and useful families of subsets in the case of finite sets.
Let
n
be a positive integer, recall that
[n]
denotes the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Definition 1.2

(Hypergraph)
.

A
hypergraphs
on
[n]
is an element
H\in\wp^{2}([n])
. The elements of
H
are called
hyperedges
.
The category of hypergraphs on
[n]
with arrows being the inclusion
H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}
, for
H_{1}
and
H_{1}
two hypergraphs, is denoted by
\mathsf{hypg}(n)
.

Definition 1.3

(Abstract simplicial complex)
.

An
(abstract) simplicial complex
on
[n]
is an element
K\in\wp^{2}([n])
such that:

•

\forall I\in K,\forall J\subseteq I,J\in K
. (downward closure)

The elements of
K
are called its
faces
.
The category of simplicial complexes on
[n]
is the subcategory of
\mathsf{hypg}(n)
restricted to simplicial complexes, and is denoted by
\mathsf{scpx}(n)
.

The
empty simplicial complex
is
\varnothing
and the
trivial simplicial complex
is
\{\varnothing\}
.

Remark 1.4
.

Throughout, we consider that the empty set is a simplicial complex since it is useful in the context of operads.
For instance, without this assumption, the maps
\operatorname{MNF}
and
\operatorname{MNU}
defined after are not well-defined.

However, in the context of polyhedral product and Stanley-Reisner theory, we require simplicial complexes to be non-empty, and we will denote by
\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing}
the category of simplicial complexes which are not empty.

Definition 1.5

(Upward complex)
.

An
upward complex
on
[n]
is an element
U\in\wp^{2}([n])
such that:

•

\forall I\in U,\forall I\subseteq J\subseteq[n],J\in U
. (upward closure)

The elements of
U
are called its
upfaces
.
The category of upward complexes on
[n]
is the subcategory of
\mathsf{hypg}(n)
restricted to upward complexes, and is denoted by
\mathsf{ucpx}(n)
.

Simplicial and upward complexes are in bijection by two involutions as follows.

Proposition 1.6
.

The following functors are involution from the category of simplicial complexes to the one of upward complexes on
[n]
:

\displaystyle\complement:\mathsf{scpx}(n)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{ucpx}(n)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\mapsto\complement K\coloneqq\wp([n])\setminus K,\text{ which is contravariant,}

and

\displaystyle\complement^{\prime}:\mathsf{scpx}(n)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{ucpx}(n)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\mapsto\complement^{\prime}K\coloneqq\{\complement I\mid I\in K\},\text{ which is covariant.}

Proof.

Let
I\in\complement K
, let
I^{\prime}\supseteq I
, if
I^{\prime}\in K
we would have
I\in K
since
I\subseteq I^{\prime}
and
K
is a simplicial complex, a contradiction.
Hence,
\complement K
is an upward complex.
The contravariance is direct.

Let
I\in\complement^{\prime}K
, so we have
I=[n]\setminus J
for some
J\in K
.
Let
I^{\prime}\supseteq I
, hence
J^{\prime}\coloneqq[n]\setminus I^{\prime}\subseteq[n]\setminus I=J
, thus
J^{\prime}\in K
since
K
is a simplicial complex, and hence
I^{\prime}=[n]\setminus J^{\prime}
is in
\complement^{\prime}K
, as desired.
Suppose that
L\subseteq K
, then for all
I\in\complement^{\prime}L\Leftrightarrow I\complement I\in L
, hence
\complement I\in K\Leftrightarrow I\in\complement^{\prime}K
. Therefore,
\complement^{\prime}L\subseteq\complement^{\prime}K
, that is the functor
\complement^{\prime}
is covariant.
∎

The last class of family of subsets we will use in this article is as follows.

Definition 1.7

(Transversal family)
.

A
transversal family
on
[n]
is an element
T\in\wp^{2}([n])
such that:

•

\forall I,J\in T,I\subseteq J\Rightarrow I=J
, (no set contains any other)

or equivalently

•

\forall I,J\in T,I\neq J\Rightarrow I\setminus J\neq\varnothing\text{ and }J\setminus I\neq\varnothing
, (no set contains any other)

The set of transversal families on
[n]
is denoted by
\mathsf{transv}(n)
.

Proposition 1.8
.

The map
\complement^{\prime}
is an involution of
\mathsf{transv}(n)
, for every
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

Let
T\in\wp^{2}(n)
.
We have:

T
is a transversal family

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\forall I,J\in T,I\neq J\Rightarrow I\setminus J\neq\varnothing\text{ and }J\setminus I\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\forall I,J\in T,\complement I\neq\complement J\Rightarrow(\complement I)\setminus(\complement J)\neq\varnothing\text{ and }(\complement J)\setminus(\complement I)\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\forall I,J\in\complement^{\prime}T,I\neq J\Rightarrow I\setminus J\neq\varnothing\text{ and }J\setminus I\neq\varnothing)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\complement^{\prime}T\text{ is a transversal family}.

∎

The set of transversal families is in bijection with both simplicial and upward complexes, as follows.
Given a simplicial complex
K
, a
facet
of
K
is an inclusionwise maximal face of
K
.
The set of
facets
of
K
, denoted by
\widehat{K}
, is a transversal family.
Similarly, given an upward complex
U
, the set of inclusionwise minimal elements of
U
, denoted by
\widecheck{U}
, is also a transversal family.
The set of
minimal non-faces
of
K
is denoted by
\operatorname{MNF}(K)=\widecheck{\left(\complement K\right)}
and is a transversal family.
Similarly, the set of
maximal non-upfaces
of an upward complex
U
is denoted by
\operatorname{MNU}(U)
, equals
\widehat{\left(\complement U\right)}
, and is a transversal family.

We denote by
{(-)}^{\downarrow}
, resp.
{(-)}^{\uparrow}
, the functor left adjoint to
\mathsf{scpx}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{hypg}
, resp.
\mathsf{ucpx}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{hypg}
.
More explicitly, given a hypergraph
H
, the
downward closure
, resp.
upward closure
, of
H
is the simplicial complex
H^{\downarrow}
which is minimal for the inclusion of simplicial complexes, resp. upward complex
H^{\uparrow}
which is minimal for the inclusion of upward complexes, that contains
H
. Finally, we obtain the commutative diagram in
Figure

1
.

\mathsf{scpx}(n)
\mathsf{ucpx}(n)
\mathsf{transv}(n)
\mathsf{transv}(n)
\mathsf{hypg}(n)
\complement\ \&\ \complement^{\prime}
\complement\ \&\ \complement^{\prime}
\widehat{(-)}
(-)^{\downarrow}
\widecheck{(-)}
(-)^{\uparrow}
\operatorname{MNF}
\operatorname{MNU}
\top
\top
(-)^{\downarrow}
(-)^{\uparrow}

Figure 1.
Commutative diagram between families of subsets of
[n]
, for every
n\geq 1
. Note that all maps are functorial, except those landing in
\mathsf{transv}(n)
.

1.3.
Reduced families of subsets

All the families of subsets defined above have a natural
reduced
variant, obtained by restricting to
\overline{\wp}^{2}([n])
.

Definition 1.9

(Reduced family)
.

A family of subsets
F\in\wp^{2}([n])
is
reduced
if it is non empty and
\varnothing\notin F
, i.e.,
F\in\overline{\wp}^{2}([n])
.
We denote by
\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}(n)
,
\overline{\mathsf{ucpx}}(n)
, and
\overline{\mathsf{transv}}(n)
the subcategories of reduced hypergraphs, upward complexes, and transversal families on
[n]
, respectively.

Definition 1.10

(Reduced simplicial complex)
.

A
reduced simplicial complex
is an element
K\in\overline{\wp}^{2}([n])
such that:

•

\forall I\in K,\forall\varnothing\neq J\subseteq I,J\in K
. (reduced downward closure)

The upward closure
{(-)}^{\uparrow}
restricts without modification to reduced families, since the upward closure of a family of nonempty sets never introduces
\varnothing
. However, the usual downward closure
{(-)}^{\downarrow}
does introduce
\varnothing
, so we define a reduced variant.

Definition 1.11

(Reduced downward closure)
.

Given a reduced hypergraph
H\in\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}(n)
, its
reduced downward closure
is

H^{\downarrow}\coloneqq\{\varnothing\neq J\subseteq I\mid I\in H\},

which is the smallest reduced simplicial complex containing
H
, and is left adjoint to the inclusion
\overline{\mathsf{scpx}}(n)\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}(n)
.

Remark 1.12
.

The reduced downward closure and the usual downward closure differ precisely by
the empty face. Among the maps in the diagram of
Figure

1
,
only
{(-)}^{\uparrow}
,
\widehat{(-)}
, and
\widecheck{(-)}
restrict directly to
the reduced subcategories. The complement operations
\complement
and
\complement^{\prime}
do not:

\complement K=\wp([n])\setminus K
always contains
\varnothing
when
K
is reduced,
and
\complement^{\prime}K
contains
\varnothing
whenever
[n]\in K
. Consequently,

\operatorname{MNF}
and
\operatorname{MNU}
do not restrict to reduced families either.

1.4.
Face posets and their categories

The Boolean lattice
(\wp([n]),\subseteq)
, viewed as a category, has subsets
of
[n]
as objects, and a unique morphism
J\to I
whenever
J\subseteq I
.

Definition 1.13

(Face category)
.

Let
H
be a (reduced) hypergraph on
[n]
.
The
face category
of
H
, denoted
\operatorname{cat}(H)
, is the full subcategory of
(\wp([n]),\subseteq)
on the objects
H\cup\{\varnothing\}
.
The
opposite face category
is
{\operatorname{cat}(H)}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

When
K
is a (reduced) simplicial complex on
[n]
, we call

\operatorname{cat}(K)
the face category of
K
; the empty set is
the initial object.

Remark 1.14
.

For a simplicial complex
K
, the opposite face category

{\operatorname{cat}(K)}^{\mathrm{op}}
coincides with the face category
of the upward complex
\complement^{\prime}K
.

1.5.
Definitions of Operads

The purpose of operads is to encode universally all the operations and their compositions acting on any category of type of algebras.
In order to do so, one encodes abstractly the set of all operations of arity
n
together with their symmetric by a set
\cal O(n)
equipped with a (right) action of the symmetric group
\mathbb{S}_{n}
.
It comes with a way of composing its elements
\cal O(m)\times\cal O(n)\to\cal O(n+m-1)
that is compatible with the action of the symmetric group, see
[
35
, Chapter 5]
for more details.
In this paper, we consider only set-theoretical operads.

Definition 1.15

(
\mathbb{S}
-collection)
.

An
\mathbb{S}
-collection is a collection of sets
\cal O={\{\cal O(n)\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
equipped with a right action of the collection of symmetric groups
\mathbb{S}\coloneqq{\{\mathbb{S}_{n}\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, that is
\cal O(n)\curvearrowleft\mathbb{S}_{n}
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 1.16

(Set-theoretical operad)
.

A
set(-theoretical) operad
is an
\mathbb{S}
-collection
\cal O={\{\cal O(n)\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, together with composition maps
\{\circ_{i}\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\circ_{i}\colon\cal O(n)\times\cal O(m)

\displaystyle\to\cal O(n+m-1)

\displaystyle(A,B)

\displaystyle\mapsto A\circ_{i}B,

for every
n\geq 1
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
i=1,\ldots,n
, which is compatible with the action of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
\mathbb{S}_{m}
, see
[
35
, Chapter 5]
.
Moreover, the composition maps must satisfy the following two conditions,
for every
(A,B,C)\in\cal O(\ell)\times\cal O(n)\times\cal O(m)
, we have:

•

(A\circ_{i}B)\circ_{j+m-1}C=(A\circ_{j}C)\circ_{i}B
, for every
1\leq i<j\leq\ell
(parallel axiom),

•

A\circ_{i}(B\circ_{j}C)=(A\circ_{i}B)\circ_{j+i-1}C
, for every
1\leq i\leq\ell,1\leq j\leq m
(sequential axiom).

An operad is
unital
when there is a distinguished element in
\cal{O}(1)
, denoted by
\id
, which satisfies
\id\circ_{1}A=A\circ_{i}\id=A
.
Removing the operation
\id
from a unital operad
\cal O
does not always yield an operad
\overline{\cal O}
.
When it is the case, we say that
\cal O
is an
augmented
unital operad, and
\cal O
is the
augmentation
of
\overline{\cal O}
.

Example 1.17
.

A first non-trivial operad is the commutative operad
\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
, which has a single operation of arity
n
with trivial
\mathbb{S}_{n}
-action, for
n\geq 1
and no operation with arity
0
.
Adding an operation of arity
0
to
\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
yields an operad that we denote by
\operatorname{\mathit{pCom}}
.

1.6.
Monoid, monads, and operads

We refer to
[
35
, Appendix E]
and
[
39
, Chapter VII]
for more details.

Definition 1.18

(Monoid)
.

Let
(\mathrm{C},\otimes,e)
be a monoidal category.
A
monoid
in
\mathrm{C}
is a triple
(M,\mu,\eta)
consisting of an object

M\in\mathrm{C}
, a
multiplication

\mu\colon M\otimes M\to M
, and a

unit

\eta\colon e\to M
, satisfying the standard unitality and associativity axioms.

Example 1.19
.

The category of
\mathbb{S}
-collections, denoted by
\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathsf{Coll}
, is equipped with the
composition product

\circ
, defined for two
\mathbb{S}
-collections
\cal O
and
\cal Q
by

(\cal O\circ\cal Q)(n)\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}\cal O(k)\times\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}\cal Q(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\cal Q(n_{k}),

with unit
\mathsf{I}
concentrated in arity
1
with
\mathsf{I}(1)=\{*\}
.
A set-theoretical operad is then equivalently a monoid in
(\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathsf{Coll},\circ,\mathsf{I})
, see
[
35
, Section 5.2]
.

Definition 1.20

(Monad)
.

A
monad
on a category
\mathrm{C}
is a triple
(\mathsf{T},\eta,\mu)
consisting of an endofunctor
\mathsf{T}\colon\mathrm{C}\to\mathrm{C}
, a
unit

\eta\colon\id_{\mathrm{C}}\to\mathsf{T}
, and a
multiplication

\mu\colon\mathsf{T}^{2}\to\mathsf{T}
, satisfying the standard unitality and associativity axioms.

Example 1.21
.

The power set functor
\wp\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
is a monad
(\wp,\eta,\mu)

with unit
\eta\colon\id_{\mathsf{Set}}\to\wp
given by
\eta(x)\coloneqq\{x\}
, and
multiplication
\mu\colon\wp^{2}\to\wp
given by
\mu(\cal A)\coloneqq\bigcup\cal A
.
The reduced power set functor
\overline{\wp}\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
is likewise a monad,
with the same unit and multiplication restricted to non-empty sets.

Definition 1.22

(Lax monoidal functor)
.

Let
(\mathrm{C},\otimes,e)
and
(\mathrm{D},\otimes^{\prime},e^{\prime})
be monoidal categories.
A
lax monoidal functor
is a functor
F\colon\mathrm{C}\to\mathrm{D}
equipped
with a natural transformation
\varphi_{X,Y}\colon F(X)\otimes^{\prime}F(Y)\to F(X\otimes Y)

and a unit map
\varphi_{0}\colon e^{\prime}\to F(e)
, satisfying the associativity and unit
coherence diagrams; see
[
35
, Appendix B.3.3]
.

Example 1.23
.

The power set functor
\wp\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
is a lax monoidal endofunctor of

(\mathsf{Set},\times,\{*\})
, with structure maps

\displaystyle\varphi_{X,Y}\colon\wp(X)\times\wp(Y)

\displaystyle\to\wp(X\times Y)

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}\colon\{*\}

\displaystyle\to\wp(\{*\})

\displaystyle(I,J)

\displaystyle\mapsto I\times J,

\displaystyle*

\displaystyle\mapsto\{*\}.

The reduced power set functor
\overline{\wp}
is likewise a lax monoidal endofunctor,
with the same structure maps restricted to non-empty sets. Both
\eta
and
\mu
are
monoidal natural transformations, making
\wp
and
\overline{\wp}

commutative
monads
in the sense of Kock
[
33
]
; see
[
33
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Finally, we have the following.

Proposition 1.24

(
[
35
, Proposition B.3.4]
)
.

The image of a monoid in a category under a lax monoidal functor is a monoid in the image category.

1.7.
Presentation of an operad

The previous definitions of an operad are given “top-to-bottom”, in the sense that we describe a global object whose elements can be composed together.
Here, we consider a “bottom-to-top” definition: we will construct the free operad generated by a given set of operations and quotient it by given relations.
An operad is an algebraic structure like many others, coming with a forgetful functor from operads to
\mathbb{S}
-collections that forgets the
\circ_{i}
compositions.
The left adjoint to this forgetful functor is called the
free operad functor
, see
[
35
, Section 5.5]
.
For a given collection
M={\{M(n)\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we call
\cal TM
the
free operad
on the collection
M
.

One canonical construction to encode the free operad is given by using rooted tree.
We denote by
\cal TM
the
free tree module on
M
.
It is the operad on rooted trees, with decorated internal nodes, defined recursively as follows.
The operations of
\cal TM
are

•

the unit operation
|\id
,

•

the atomic corollas with
n
leaves, each of them with internal node decorated by an element of
M(n)
, or

•

(finite) trees having one root corolla, say
C_{R}
, and each elements grafted to the leaves of
C_{R}
are elements of
\cal TM
, with each internal node decorated by an element of
M
, up to the action of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
.

A
relation
between trees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, decorated by elements of
M
, with the same arity is written as
T_{1}\equiv T_{2}
.
Given a tree
T
of
\cal TM
and a relation
T_{1}\equiv T_{2}
, if
T_{1}
is a subtree of
T
, then, the
rewriting

T_{1}\rightarrow T_{2}
in
T
is obtained by replacing
T_{1}
by
T_{2}
in
T
. The
quotient
of the tree module
\cal TM
by a set of relations
R
is denoted by
\cal TM/\langle R\rangle
, and is obtained by the quotient with the following equivalence relation: two trees
T
and
T^{\prime}
are equivalent if and only if there is a sequence of rewriting from
T
to
T^{\prime}
using the relations in
R
.
When an operad
\cal O
is isomorphic to a quotient
\cal TM/\langle R\rangle
, we call the latter a
presentation
of
\cal O
.

Example 1.25
.

The commutative operad
\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
is presented by the free operad on a single binary operation
\mu
modulo the relation
\mu\circ_{1}\mu\equiv\mu\circ_{2}\mu
.

The operad
\operatorname{\mathit{pCom}}
is presented by the free operad on a binary operation
\mu
and a nullary operation
\bullet
modulo the relations
\mu\circ_{1}\mu\equiv\mu\circ_{2}\mu
and
\mu\circ_{1}\bullet\equiv\mu\circ_{2}\bullet\equiv\id
.

1.8.
A motivated example: the permutative operad

Definition 1.26

(Permutative operad
[
14
]
)
.

The
permutative operad
, denoted by
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
, is a set theoretical operad without any operation of arity
0
and whose set of operations of arity
n\geq 1
has
n
elements, denoted by
1,2,\ldots,n
, that is:

\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}(0)=\varnothing\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}(n)\coloneqq[n],\text{ for }n\geq 1.

The
\mathbb{S}
-collection structure is the natural action of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
on
[n]
.
(Note that, for distinct
n
and
m
, the operations
i
of arity
n
and
m
are different. We omit this in our notations for the sake of convenience.) For
k=1,\ldots,n
, the composition of
(i,j)\in\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}(n)\times\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}(m)
is given by:

i\circ_{k}j=\begin{cases}i+m-1&\text{ if }k<i,\\
i+j-1&\text{ if }k=i,\\
i&\text{ if }k>i.\end{cases}

A graphical illustration is given in
Figure

2
.

k<i

3=\square\square\blacksquare\square\square
2=\underbrace{\square\blacksquare\square}
\downarrow\circ_{2}

\rightarrow

3\circ_{2}2=\square\underbrace{\square\square\square}\blacksquare\square\square=5

k=i

3=\square\square\blacksquare\square\square
2=\underbrace{\square\blacksquare\square}
\downarrow\circ_{3}

\rightarrow

3\circ_{3}2=\square\square\underbrace{\square\blacksquare\square}\square\square=4

k>i

3=\square\square\blacksquare\square\square
2=\underbrace{\square\blacksquare\square}
\downarrow\circ_{4}

\rightarrow

3\circ_{4}2=\square\square\blacksquare\underbrace{\square\square\square}\square=3

Figure 2.
An illustration of the compositions of two elements of
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
. The element
i\in\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}(n)
is represented by a sequence of
n
squares, where only the
i
th one is colored in black.

[

]
)

, then, any sequence of compositions of these two generators yields a binary tree with a unique leaf connected by a bold path from the root. The permutative relations, in order of appearance, correspond to the bold path ending at the left leaf, the right leaf, and the middle leaf, respectively.
∎

1.9.
Algebra over an operad

To every set
X
, there is a canonical operad
\operatorname{\mathit{End}}_{X}
, whose operations of arity
n
are given by:

\operatorname{\mathit{End}}_{X}(n)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{Set}}(X^{n},X),

with the action of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
obtained by permuting the entries.
The compositions are given by

\displaystyle f\circ_{i}g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},

\displaystyle y_{1},\ldots,y_{m},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1},g(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}),x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n}),

for
n\geq 1,m\in\mathbb{N},f\in\operatorname{\mathit{End}}_{X}(n),g\in\operatorname{\mathit{End}}_{X}(m)
, and
x_{1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{i}\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots y_{m}\in X
.

Given an operad
\cal O
, an
\cal O
-algebra
structure on
X
is a morphism of operads
\cal z\colon\cal O\to\operatorname{\mathit{End}}_{X}
.
Equivalently, it is described by a sequence of maps
\cal z={(\cal z_{n})}_{n\geq 1}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rll}\cal z_{n}\colon&\cal{O}(n)\times X^{n}&\to X\\
&(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\mapsto\cal z_{n}(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\end{array}

which are compatible with the right action of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
on
\cal O(n)
and the left action of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
on
X^{n}
(obtained by permuting the entries).

2.
Power set operads: definition, examples, properties

In this section, we study how the power set functor behaves with respect to set operads.

2.1.
Power set operad functor

Definition 2.1

(Power of a collection)
.

Given an
\mathbb{S}
-collection
\cal O={\{\cal O(n)\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, its
power collection
is:

\wp\cal O={\{\wp\cal O(n)\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\coloneqq{\{\wp(\cal O(n))\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},

with the natural induced action of
\mathbb{S}
.
We define the reduced power collection
\overline{\wp}\cal O
in the same way.

We can iterate the (reduced) power set maps.

Definition 2.2

(Iterated power of a collection)
.

Let
\cal O
be an
\mathbb{S}
-collection.
We say an
\mathbb{S}
-collection
\cal Q
is
based on
\cal O
with power

k\geq 0
when it is is a subcollection of
\wp^{k}\cal O
. By convention
\wp^{0}\cal O=\cal O
.

Proposition 2.3
.

The endofunctors
\wp
and
\overline{\wp}
are lax monoidal in the monoidal category

(\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathsf{Coll},\circ,\mathsf{I})
.

Proof.

We construct the lax monoidal structure maps. The unit map

\varphi_{0}\colon\mathsf{I}\to\wp\mathsf{I}
is given by
*\mapsto\{*\}
in arity
1
.

For the natural transformation, we need to construct, for every two
\mathbb{S}
-collections

\cal O
and
\cal Q
, a morphism

\varphi_{\cal O,\cal Q}\colon(\wp\cal O)\circ(\wp\cal Q)\longrightarrow\wp(\cal O\circ\cal Q).

At arity
n
, an element of
((\wp\cal O)\circ(\wp\cal Q))(n)
consists of a tuple

(A;B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
where
k\geq 0
,
A\in\wp(\cal O(k))
,

B_{j}\in\wp(\cal Q(n_{j}))
for
j\in[k]
,
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=n
, and all
n_{i}\geq 0
.
We define

\varphi_{\cal O,\cal Q}(A;B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})\coloneqq\bigl\{\,p\circ(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})\mid p\in A,\,q_{j}\in B_{j}\text{ for }j\in[k]\,\bigr\}\;\in\;\wp\bigl((\cal O\circ\cal Q)(n)\bigr),

where
p\circ(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
denotes the composition in
\cal O\circ\cal Q
.
This is exactly the image of
(A;B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
under the iterated application of the
lax monoidal structure map
\varphi_{X,Y}(I,J)=I\times J
of
\wp
on
(\mathsf{Set},\times,\{*\})

from
Example

1.23
, applied to each factor of the product

\cal O(k)\times\cal Q(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\cal Q(n_{k})
.

The associativity and unit coherence axioms for
(\varphi_{\cal O,\cal Q},\varphi_{0})

follow from those of
\wp
on
(\mathsf{Set},\times,\{*\})
together with the associativity of the
composition product
\circ
. The case of
\overline{\wp}
is identical, noting that

A\neq\varnothing
and
B_{j}\neq\varnothing
for all
j
imply

\varphi_{\cal O,\cal Q}(A;B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})\neq\varnothing
.
∎

By
Proposition

2.3
and
Proposition

1.24
, the (reduced) power set is an endofunctor in the category of set operads
\mathsf{Op}(\mathsf{Set})
, as follows.

Definition 2.4

(Power set operad functor)
.

The
power set operad functor
is the following endofunctor:

\displaystyle\wp\colon\mathsf{Op}(\mathsf{Set})

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Op}(\mathsf{Set})

\displaystyle(\cal O,\{\circ_{i}\})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\wp\cal O,\{\circ^{\wp}_{i}\}),

where the compositions
\circ^{\wp}_{i}
, are as follows:

\displaystyle\circ^{\wp}_{i}\colon\wp\cal O(n)\times\wp\cal{O}(m)

\displaystyle\to\wp\cal{O}(n+m-1)

\displaystyle(A,B)

\displaystyle\mapsto A\circ_{i}^{\wp}B\coloneqq\{a\circ_{i}b\mid a\in A,b\in B\}.

We call
(\wp\cal O,\{\circ^{\wp}_{i}\})
the
power
of the operad
(\cal O,\{\circ_{i}\})
.
Similarly, we denote by
\overline{\wp}
the reduced power set operad functor, which produces the
reduced power
operad
(\overline{\wp}\cal O,\{\circ^{\overline{\wp}}_{i}\})
.

Remark 2.5
.

The functor also works with nonsymmetric operads. We focus here on symmetric operads.

Remark 2.6
.

The functor
\wp
only adds the empty set to each arity of the
\mathbb{S}
-collections obtained from
\overline{\wp}
, which creates a distinguished
\mathbb{S}
-collection.
For convenience, we introduce the
\mathbb{S}
-collection of empty sets
{\{\varnothing_{n}\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, one for each arity
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By definition, when either
A
or
B
is the empty set, the composition
A\circ_{i}^{\wp}B
produces the empty set.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{O},\{\circ_{i}\})
be a set operad. Then:

(1)

The
\mathbb{S}
-collection
{\{\varnothing_{n}\}}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
equipped with
\{\circ_{i}^{\wp}\}
forms a non-unital ideal suboperad of
(\wp\mathcal{O},\{\circ_{i}^{\wp}\})
, isomorphic to
\operatorname{\mathit{pCom}}
as a non-unital operad.

(2)

The suboperad
\overline{\wp}\mathcal{O}\hookrightarrow\wp\mathcal{O}
is unital, contains the unit
\{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{O}}\}
of
\wp\mathcal{O}
, and provides a canonical splitting. In particular, there is a short exact sequence of non-unital operads

(2.1)

0\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathit{pCom}}\longrightarrow\wp\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\overline{\wp}\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow 0,

which splits in the category of non-unital operads, yielding a decomposition
\wp\mathcal{O}(n)=\overline{\wp}\mathcal{O}(n)\sqcup\{\varnothing_{n}\}
at each arity.

Proof.

For the first point, we check that
\{\varnothing_{n}\}
is closed under
\{\circ_{i}^{\wp}\}
: indeed
\varnothing_{n}\circ_{i}^{\wp}\varnothing_{m}=\varnothing_{n+m-1}
, and the ideal property follows from
\varnothing_{n}\circ_{i}^{\wp}A=B\circ_{j}\varnothing_{m}=\varnothing_{n+m-1}
for any
A\in\wp\mathcal{O}(m)
and
B\in\wp\mathcal{O}(n)
. The
\mathbb{S}
-collection structure is trivial since there is exactly one element at each arity. The second point is a direct consequence of
Definition

2.4
. The unit of
\wp\mathcal{O}
is
\{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{O}}\}\in\overline{\wp}\mathcal{O}(1)
, confirming that the splitting does not extend to unital operads.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

Recall that in
\wp\cal O
, we have a bijection
\complement
, and hence we can introduce the following other composition, which is the transport of
\{\circ^{\wp}_{i}\}
by
\complement
and yields a different functor isomorphic to
\wp
, with the following formula.

\displaystyle\circ^{c\wp}_{i}\colon\wp\cal O(n)\times\wp\cal{O}(m)

\displaystyle\to\wp\cal{O}(n+m-1)

\displaystyle(A,B)

\displaystyle\mapsto A\circ_{i}^{c\wp}B\coloneqq\complement\{a\circ_{i}b\mid a\in\complement A,b\in\complement B\}.

Proposition 2.9

(
[
33
, Theorem 2.3]
,
[
44
, Theorem 3.6]
)
.

Let
(M,\eta,\mu)
be a monad on a monoidal category
(\mathrm{C},\otimes,e)
such that
M
is a lax monoidal functor and both
\eta
and
\mu
are monoidal natural transformations. Then
M
lifts to a monad on the category of monoids
\mathsf{Mon}(\mathrm{C},\otimes,e)
.

Such a monad is called a
monoidal monad

[
33
]
.
Since a set-theoretical operad is equivalently a monoid in

(\mathbb{S}\text{-}\mathsf{Coll},\circ,\mathsf{I})
, applying

Proposition

2.9
and
Proposition

1.24
to
\wp
and
\overline{\wp}
immediately yields a monad on
\mathsf{Op}(\mathsf{Set})
.
More explicitly, the
unit
and
product
are two operations on the endofunctors of
\mathsf{Op}(\mathsf{Set})
given by

\begin{array}[]{rrl}\eta_{\mathcal{O}}\colon&\mathcal{O}&\to\wp\mathcal{O}\\
&a&\mapsto\{a\},\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{rrl}\mu_{\mathcal{O}}\colon&\wp^{2}\mathcal{O}&\to\wp\mathcal{O}\\
&\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}\}&\mapsto\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}A_{i},\end{array}

for every set operad
(\cal O,\{\circ_{i}\})
.

Corollary 2.10
.

The unit
\eta
and the product
\mu
endow the endofunctors
\wp
and
\overline{\wp}
with a monad structure.

Remark 2.11
.

Note that the map
\eta
includes
\cal O
as a suboperad of
\wp\cal O
.

Definition 2.12

(Augmentation of an operad)
.

A morphism of operads
\rho\colon\wp\cal O\to\cal O
is called an
augmentation
.

Remark 2.13
.

In the case when there is an augmentation, the successive iterations of
\wp
have a simplicial structure and we can consider its simplicial bar construction
[
25
]
. It would be interesting to study the homology or homotopy of this bar construction.

In the rest of the article, for the sake of simplifying notations, we will denote the compositions
\{\circ^{\wp}_{i}\}
by
\{\circ_{i}\}
.

2.2.
Examples of reduced power one set operads

Let us apply the reduced power set functor to the set operads corresponding to some of the operads from the literature.

Trivial operad

The trivial operad
\operatorname{\mathit{I}}
consists only of the operation
\id
of arity
1
. We have
\overline{\wp}\operatorname{\mathit{I}}(1)=\{\{\id\}\}
, and
\{\id\}\circ_{1}\{\id\}=\{\id\}
. Hence,
\overline{\wp}\operatorname{\mathit{I}}\cong\operatorname{\mathit{I}}
.

Commutative operad

Recall that the commutative operad
\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
has a single operation of arity
n
with trivial
\mathbb{S}_{n}
-action, for every
n\geq 1
.
Hence, we have
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Com}})\cong\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
.
Similarly,
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{pCom}})\cong\operatorname{\mathit{pCom}}
.

Before moving to the next case, we define some convenient notations.
For
I\subseteq[n]
, and
k\in[n]
, we define
I^{<k}\coloneqq\{i\in I\mid i<k\}
and
I^{>k}\coloneqq\{i\in I\mid i>k\}
.
Moreover, for an integer
\ell\geq 0
, we define
I+\ell\coloneqq\{i+\ell\mid i\in I\}
.

Permutative operad

Let us now apply the functors
\overline{\wp}
to the operad
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
.

We have
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}(n))=\overline{\wp}([n])
, for
n\geq 1
.
Let
(I,J)\in\overline{\wp}([n])\times\overline{\wp}([m])
, and let
k\in[n]
, we have:

\displaystyle I\circ_{k}J=

\displaystyle\{i\circ_{k}j\mid i\in I,j\in J\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{i\circ_{k}j\mid i\in I,j\in J,i<k\}\sqcup\{i\circ_{k}j\mid i\in I,j\in J,i=k\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{i\circ_{k}j\mid i\in I,j\in J,i>k\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{i\mid i\in I,i<k\}\sqcup\{k+j-1\mid k\in I,j\in J\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{i+m-1\mid i\in I,i>k\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}I^{<k}\sqcup(J+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)&\text{when }k\in I,\\
I^{<k}\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)&\text{when }k\notin I.\\
\end{cases}

An illustration of the composition in
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
is given in
Figure

3
.

\square\blacksquare\blacksquare\square\blacksquare
\underbrace{\blacksquare\square\blacksquare}
y=
\circ_{3}
x=

\rightarrow

x\circ_{3}y=\square\blacksquare\underbrace{\blacksquare\square\blacksquare}\square\blacksquare

\square\blacksquare\blacksquare\square\blacksquare\phantom{\square\square}
\underbrace{\blacksquare\square\blacksquare}
y=
\circ_{4}
x=

\rightarrow

x\circ_{4}y=\square\blacksquare\blacksquare\underbrace{\square\square\square}\blacksquare

Figure 3.
An illustration of the composition in
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
. The elements of a subset are colored in black and the elements of the complementary are in white. Inserting in a white square “forgets the information”.

Proposition 2.14
.

The operad
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
is generated by three binary operations
\hbox to27.01pt{\vbox to17.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.89046pt\lower-11.17824pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

where, the transposition
(12)
sends
\{1\}
to
\{2\}
and
\{1,2\}
to itself, respectively.

Proof.

We consider the correspondence of non-empty subsets of
[n]
with binary words having at least one nonzero letter, the proof is then the same as the proof of Giraudo
[
24
, Proposition 4.24]

∎

This operad is known in the literature as the commutative triassociative operad
[
48
]
. Its nonsymmetric version, the triassociative operad was introduced by Loday and Ronco in
[
34
]
.
It has reemerged recently in the work of Mermoud and Roca i Lucio
[
42
]
as they showed that it governs the algebraic structure of cooperative games.

Definition 2.15

(Commutative triassociative algebra
[
48
,
34
]
)
.

The
commutative triassociative operad
, denoted by
\operatorname{\mathit{ComTrias}}
, is the set theoretical operad
\cal T\left(\dashv,\vdash,\perp\right)/\langle R\rangle
, where
R
is made up of
11
relations between the binary operations
\dashv,\vdash
, and
\perp
defined in
[
34
, Section 1.2]
which are the exact same relations satisfied by

, respectively.

Since the operad
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
has the same presentation as
\operatorname{\mathit{ComTrias}}
we obtain the following.

Proposition 2.16
.

We have

\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})\cong\operatorname{\mathit{ComTrias}},

seen as set operads.

We now define a functor of interest that will play an important role in the algebras we will construct over power set operads.

Definition 2.17

(Cubical functor)
.

Let
(C,\otimes,e)
be a monoidal category,

n\geq 1
, and
\underline{f}\coloneqq{(f_{i}\colon Y_{i}\to X_{i})}_{i\in[n]}
a sequence
of morphisms of
\mathrm{C}
.
The
cubical functor
is given by

\cal z_{n}(-;\underline{f})\;\colon\;(\wp([n]),\subseteq)\longrightarrow\mathrm{C}

by setting, on objects,

\cal z_{n}(I;\underline{f})\;\coloneqq\;\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{I},\quad Z_{i}^{I}\coloneqq\begin{cases}X_{i}&i\in I,\\
Y_{i}&i\notin I,\end{cases}

and on a morphism
I\subseteq J
,

\cal z_{n}(I\subseteq J;\underline{f})\;\coloneqq\;\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}^{I,J},\qquad\phi_{i}^{I,J}\;\coloneqq\;\begin{cases}\mathrm{id}_{X_{i}}&i\in I,\\
f_{i}&i\in J\setminus I,\\
\mathrm{id}_{Y_{i}}&i\notin J.\end{cases}

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
(C,\otimes,e)
be a small strict symmetric monoidal category.
Then, the set
\mor(\mathrm{C})
of all arrows of
\mathrm{C}
carries a

\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
-algebra structure given by:

\zeta_{n}\colon\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})(n)\times{\mor(\mathrm{C})}^{n}\to\mor(\mathrm{C}),\qquad\zeta_{n}(I;\underline{f})\;\coloneqq\;\cal z_{n}(\varnothing\subseteq I;\underline{f}).

Proof.

Let
\underline{f}={(f_{j})}_{j\in[n+m-1]}
,
\underline{f}_{[k,k+m-1]}={(f_{j+k-1})}_{j\in[m]}

the subsequence at position
k
, and
\underline{h}={(h_{j})}_{j\in[n]}
defined by

h_{j}=f_{j}
for
j<k
,
h_{k}=\zeta_{m}(J;\underline{f}_{[k,k+m-1]})
, and
h_{j}=f_{j+m-1}
for
j>k
.
We must show the algebra axiom:

\zeta_{n+m-1}(I\circ_{k}J;\,\underline{f})\;=\;\zeta_{n}(I;\,\underline{h}).

Since
\otimes
is a bifunctor, both sides factor over the three index
blocks
[k-1]
,
[k,k+m-1]
,
[k+m,n+m-1]
.

Case
k\in I
.

Here
I\circ_{k}J=I^{<k}\sqcup(J+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)
, so:

\zeta_{n+m-1}(I\circ_{k}J;\,\underline{f})\;=\;\zeta_{k-1}(I^{<k};\,\underline{f}_{[k-1]})\;\otimes\;\zeta_{m}(J;\,\underline{f}_{[k,k+m-1]})\;\otimes\;\zeta_{n-k}(I^{>k};\,\underline{f}_{[k+m,n+m-1]}).

Since
k\in I
, the factor
h_{k}=\zeta_{m}(J;\underline{f}_{[k,k+m-1]})

is applied in
\zeta_{n}(I;\underline{h})
, yielding the same expression.

Case
k\notin I
.

Here
I\circ_{k}J=I^{<k}\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)
, so the middle block
contributes
\mathrm{id}_{\cal z_{m}(\varnothing;\,\underline{f}_{[k,k+m-1]})}
, giving:

\zeta_{n+m-1}(I\circ_{k}J;\,\underline{f})\;=\;\zeta_{k-1}(I^{<k};\,\underline{f}_{[k-1]})\;\otimes\;\mathrm{id}_{\cal z_{m}(\varnothing;\,\underline{f}_{[k,k+m-1]})}\;\otimes\;\zeta_{n-k}(I^{>k};\,\underline{f}_{[k+m,n+m-1]}).

Since
k\notin I
, the factor
h_{k}
appears as

\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{dom}(h_{k})}=\mathrm{id}_{\cal z_{m}(\varnothing;\,\underline{f}_{[k,k+m-1]})}

in
\zeta_{n}(I;\underline{h})
, and both sides coincide.
∎

2.3.
Examples of unreduced power one set operads

Trivial operad

We have
\wp\operatorname{\mathit{I}}(0)=\{\varnothing_{0}\}
,
\wp\operatorname{\mathit{I}}(1)=\{\varnothing_{1},\{\id\}\}
, and
\wp\operatorname{\mathit{I}}(n)=\{\varnothing_{n}\}
for
n\geq 2
.
The compositions are
\varnothing_{n}\circ_{i}\varnothing_{m}=\varnothing_{n+m-1}
.

Commutative operad

We have
\wp\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}(0)=\{\varnothing_{0}\}
and
\wp\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}(n)=\{\varnothing_{n},\{\mu_{n}\}\}
, for every
n\geq 1
.
For
n,m\geq 1
, and
1\leq n
, the compositions are
\varnothing_{n}\circ_{i}\varnothing_{m}=\{\mu_{n}\}\circ_{i}\varnothing_{m}=\varnothing_{n}\circ_{i}\{\mu_{m}\}=\varnothing_{n+m-1}
, and
\{\mu_{n}\}\circ_{i}\{\mu_{m}\}=\{\mu_{n+m-1}\}
.

Permutative operad

Now, we consider
\wp\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
, which is presented as follows.

,

where the transposition
(12)
sends
\{1\}
to
\{2\}
and
\{1,2\}
to itself, respectively.

Proof.

For every arity
n\geq 1
,
\wp(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})(n)
has one more element than
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})(n)
, which is
\varnothing_{n}
, and whose composition to the left or to the right of any subset
I\subset[n]
yields an empty set, by definition.
The subsets
\{1\},\{2\},\{1,2\}\subseteq[2]
are generators since they are needed to generate the non-empty subsets of
[n]
, for every
n\geq 1
, as in the case of
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
.
The relations in
R
are unchanged since they involve only non-empty subsets.
The next relations come from the fact that
A\circ_{i}\varnothing_{0}=\varnothing_{n}
for every
A\in\wp(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})(n+1)
, and every
i\in[n+1]
.
∎

2.4.
The special case of the permutative operad

Substitution

A key feature of
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
is that
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}(n)=[n]
, so that the operation
i\in[n]
can be identified with its unique input. In particular, we get the following.

Remark 2.20
.

The composition
i\circ_{k}j
is independent of
j
whenever

i\neq k
, which gives the power set composition the unified form

(2.2)

I\circ_{k}J=\begin{cases}I^{<k}\sqcup(J+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)&\text{if }k\in I,\\
I^{<k}\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)&\text{if }k\notin I,\\
\end{cases}

valid for all
J\in\wp([m])
, including
J=\varnothing
.

Proposition 2.21
.

The
\mathbb{S}
-collection
{(\wp(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})(n))}_{n\geq 1}
with the compositions
\{\circ_{k}\}
of
Equation

2.3

forms an operad that we denote by
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}
.

Proof.

This operad coincides with
\operatorname{\mathit{ComTrias}}
on
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
when we do not consider the empty sets. A straightforward computation shows that the compositions with empty sets also satisfy the parallel and sequential axioms.
∎

.

The operad
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}
has the following presentation:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

such that the transposition
(12)
sends
\{1,2\}
to itself.

Proof.

The relations satisfied by
|\varnothing_{1}
and
\{1,2\}
are straightforward.
It is obvious that we can express
[n]
by
n-1
composition of
\{1,2\}
.
An element of
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}(n)
corresponds to a subset
I\subseteq[n]
.
Say that
\complement I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}
, then we have:

I=(\ldots(([n]\circ_{i_{1}}\varnothing_{1})\circ_{i_{2}}\varnothing_{1})\circ_{i_{3}}\ldots)\circ_{i_{k}}\varnothing_{1},

That is,
I
is obtained by composing
[n]
with
\varnothing_{1}
at every
j\notin I
.
∎

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
C=(C,\otimes,e)
be a small strict symmetric monoidal category.
Then, the collection
\mor(\mathrm{C})
of all arrows of
\mathrm{C}
up carries an
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}
-algebra structure, defined in the same way as
Proposition

2.18
.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as the one for
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})
.
∎

Composition

Recall that we have the following bijection
\complement\colon\wp([n])\to\wp([n])
.

Remark 2.24
.

By
Remark

2.8
, the transport of
\{\circ_{i}\}
through
\complement
yields another isomorpic operad on the
\mathbb{S}
-collection
(\wp([n])
, with following composition for every
I\in\wp([n]),J\in\wp([m])
, and
k\in[n]
:

(2.3)

I\circ_{k}^{c}J=\begin{cases}I^{<k}\sqcup([m]+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)&\text{if }k\in I,\\
I^{<k}\sqcup(J+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)&\text{if }k\notin I.\\
\end{cases}

We denote this operad by
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}^{c}
.

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
C=(C,\otimes,e)
be a small strict symmetric monoidal category.
Then, the collection
\mor(\mathrm{C})
of all arrows of
\mathrm{C}
up carries an
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}^{c}
-algebra structure given by:

\xi_{n}\colon\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}^{c}(n)\times{\mor(\mathrm{C})}^{n}\to\mor(\mathrm{C}),\qquad\xi_{n}(I;\underline{f})\;\coloneqq\;\cal z_{n}(I\subseteq[n];\underline{f}).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
Proposition

2.18
.
∎

3.
Simplicial complex operads and their algebras

We are now interested in power two set operads: those obtained after two iterations of the (reduced) power set functor.
In particular, we obtain composition on objects which are families of subsets, such as hypergraphs, simplicial complexes, upward complexes, and transversal families.
We focus on operads based on
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
at power two, and those based on
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}
and
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}^{c}
at power one.

3.1.
Reduced power two of
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
: reduced substitution operads

We now iterate the reduced power set functor on
\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}
and study the resulting operads on combinatorial objects.
Recall that
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})(n)=\overline{\wp}([n])
is the collection of nonempty subsets of
[n]
.
Applying
\overline{\wp}
once more, we obtain that
\overline{\wp}^{2}(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}})(n)=\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}(n)
.
We denote this operad by
(\overline{\mathsf{hypg}},\{\circ_{k}\})
, where for
K\in\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}(n)
,
L\in\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}(m)
, and
k\in[n]
, the composition is given by
Definition

2.4
as follows:

(3.1)

K\circ_{k}L=\{I^{<k}\sqcup(J+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)\mid I\in K,J\in L,k\in I\}\cup\{I^{<k}\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)\mid I\in K,k\notin I\},

and its identity is
\{\{1\}\}\in\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}(1)
.
Note that this is precisely the substitution of
L
into
K
at vertex
k
as defined in
[
1
]
, but in the case of reduced hypergraphs.
We first have.

Proposition 3.1
.

The pairs
(\overline{\mathsf{transv}},\{\circ_{k}\})
and
(\overline{\mathsf{scpx}},\{\circ_{k}\})
form suboperads of
(\overline{\mathsf{hypg}},\{\circ_{k}\})
.

Proof.

Let
K\in\overline{\mathsf{transv}}(n)
and
L\in\overline{\mathsf{transv}}(m)
, and suppose
I_{1}\circ_{k}J_{1}\subseteq I_{2}\circ_{k}J_{2}
for some
I_{1},I_{2}\in K
and
J_{1},J_{2}\in L
. We show this forces
I_{1}=I_{2}
and
J_{1}=J_{2}
, hence
K\circ_{k}L
is a reduced transversal system.
Since the ranges of the three pieces
I^{<k}
,
(J+k-1)
, and
(I^{>k}+m-1)
partition into
[k-1]
,
[k,k+m-1]
, and
[k+m,n+m-1]
respectively, the inclusion decomposes by range:

•

If
k\notin I_{1}
and
k\notin I_{2}
, then
I_{1}^{<k}\sqcup(I_{1}^{>k}+m-1)\subseteq I_{2}^{<k}\sqcup(I_{2}^{>k}+m-1)
, which gives
I_{1}\subseteq I_{2}
, hence
I_{1}=I_{2}
.

•

If
k\in I_{1}
and
k\in I_{2}
, then comparing each piece separately gives
I_{1}^{<k}\subseteq I_{2}^{<k}
,
J_{1}\subseteq J_{2}
, and
I_{1}^{>k}\subseteq I_{2}^{>k}
, hence
I_{1}\subseteq I_{2}
and
J_{1}\subseteq J_{2}
, so
I_{1}=I_{2}
and
J_{1}=J_{2}
.

•

If
k\in I_{1}
and
k\notin I_{2}
, then
J_{1}+k-1
contributes elements in
[k,k+m-1]
to the left-hand side, but the right-hand side
I_{2}^{<k}\sqcup(I_{2}^{>k}+m-1)
has no elements in this range. Since
J_{1}\neq\varnothing
, this is a contradiction.

The case
k\notin I_{1}
,
k\in I_{2}
is symmetric to the last case.

It is easily verified from
Equation

3.1
that
\circ_{k}
preserves reduced simplicial complexes.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

The substitution composition does not preserve upward closure in general: for instance
\{\{1\},\{2\},\{1,2\}\}\circ_{2}\{\{1,2\}\}=\{\{1\},\{2,3\},\{1,2,3\}\}
is not upward-closed since
\{1,2\}
and
\{1,3\}
are missing.

However, since the upward closure
{(-)}^{\uparrow}
restricts to
\overline{\mathsf{hypg}}
without modification, we may transport the operad structure via:

K\circ_{k}^{\uparrow}L\coloneqq{(K\circ_{k}L)}^{\uparrow}.

Proposition 3.3
.

The pair
(\overline{\mathsf{ucpx}},\{\circ_{k}^{\uparrow}\})
forms an operad.

Proof.

Via the bijection
M=\widecheck{(-)}:\overline{\mathsf{ucpx}}\to\overline{\mathsf{transv}}
, we have for
U,V\in\overline{\mathsf{ucpx}}
:

M(U\circ_{k}^{\uparrow}V)=M({(U\circ_{k}V)}^{\uparrow})=M(M(U)\circ_{k}M(V)),

where the last term is a transversal family by the previous proposition applied to

M(U),M(V)\in\overline{\mathsf{transv}}
. The operad axioms for
\{\circ_{k}^{\uparrow}\}
then follow
from those of
\{\circ_{k}\}
via this bijection.
∎

Note that in the case of reduced families, we cannot use the functors
\complement
and
\complement^{\prime}
.

3.2.
Power one of
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}
: substitution operads

We now consider the unreduced case.
Note that the formula
Equation

3.1
works for the two operads
\wp(\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}})
and
\overline{\wp}(\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}})
, which corresponds to hypergraphs and non-empty hypergraphs, respectively.

Remark 3.4
.

First, note that contrary to the reduced case, the composition is not stable in the
\mathbb{S}
-collection of (non-empty) transversal families.
Indeed, we have
\{\{1,2\},\{2,3\}\}\circ_{3}\{\varnothing\}=\{\{1,2\},\{2\}\}
which is not a transversal family.

However, we have a suboperad in the case of (non-empty) simplicial complexes.

Proposition 3.5
.

The pair
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{k}\})
forms a suboperad of
(\mathsf{hypg},\{\circ_{k}\})
, with unit
\pm\coloneqq\{\varnothing,\{1\}\}
.
The pair
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{k}\})
forms a suboperad of
(\mathsf{hypg}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{k}\})
, with unit
\pm
.

Proof.

It is easily seen that the unit is
\{\varnothing,\{1\}\}
.
The only difference with the proof of
Proposition

3.1
is when we compose with the empty simplicial complex, which always yields the empty simplicial complex (on a possibly bigger vertex set), and when composing with each trivial simplicial complex
\{\varnothing\}
on the vertex set
[n]
, for
n\geq 1
.
For
K\in\mathsf{scpx}(m)
, have
\{\varnothing\}\circ_{k}K=\{\varnothing\}
, and
K\circ_{k}\{\varnothing\}
is the restriction of
K
to
[m]\setminus\{k\}
, to which vertices
i\in[k+1,m]
are relabeled to
i+n-1
, which is still a simplicial complex.
∎

3.3.
Power one of
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}^{c}
: composition operads

We still denote by
\{\circ^{c}_{k}\}
the compositions obtained after taking the power of the compositions
\{\circ^{c}_{k}\}
in
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}^{c}
, and call it the
composition
of hypergraphs, as follows, for
H_{1}\in\mathsf{hypg}(n)
,
H_{2}\in\mathsf{hypg}(m)
, and
k\in[n]
:

\begin{array}[]{rl}H_{1}\circ^{c}_{k}H_{2}=&\{I\circ^{c}_{k}J\mid I\in H_{1},J\in H_{2}\}\\
=&\{I^{<k}\sqcup(J+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)\mid I\in H_{1},J\in H_{2},k\notin I\}\\
&\sqcup\{I^{<k}\sqcup([m]+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)\mid I\in H_{1},k\in I\}.\\
\end{array}

Note that its unit is
\{\varnothing\}
on the vertex set
[1]
.

Proposition 3.6
.

The pair
(\mathsf{ucpx},\{\circ^{c}_{i}\})
forms a suboperad of
(\mathsf{hypg},\{\circ^{c}_{i}\})
with unit
\{\varnothing,\{1\}\}
.

Proof.

Simply note that
\complement^{\prime}(\mathsf{ucpx},\{\circ^{c}_{i}\})=(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
, and since
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
is a suboperad of
(\mathsf{hypg},\{\circ_{i}\})
, the pair
(\mathsf{ucpx},\{\circ^{c}_{i}\})
is a suboperad of
(\mathsf{hypg},\{\circ^{c}_{i}\})
.
∎

Up to downward closing, we recover the formula for the composition of
L
in
K
at vertex
k
, as defined by Ayzenberg in
[
5
]
in the case of simplicial complexes, as follows.

(3.2)

\begin{array}[]{rl}(K\circ^{c}_{k}L)^{\downarrow}&={\{I\circ^{c}_{k}J\mid I\in K,J\in L\}}^{\downarrow}\\
&=\{I^{<k}\sqcup(J+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)\mid I\in K,J\in L,k\notin I\}\\
&\sqcup\ \{I^{<k}\sqcup(A+k-1)\sqcup(I^{>k}+m-1)\mid I\in K,A\subseteq[m],k\in I\}.\\
\end{array}

We still denote by
\circ^{c}_{i}
the composition
{(-\circ^{c}_{i}-)}^{\downarrow}
on the category of simplicial complexes.

Proposition 3.7
.

The pair
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ^{c}_{i}\})
is an operad.

Finally, we have the following diagram of operads:
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
(\mathsf{ucpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
(\mathsf{ucpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
(\mathsf{hypg},\{\circ_{i}\})
(\mathsf{hypg},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
\complement^{\prime}
\complement^{\prime}
\complement^{\prime}
\complement^{\prime}
\complement^{\prime}
\complement^{\prime}

Proposition 3.8
.

The transport of
\{\circ_{i}\}
by the bijection
\widehat{(-)}\colon\mathsf{scpx}\to\mathsf{transv}
, denoted by
\{\hat{\circ}_{i}\}
, yields an operad
(\mathsf{transv},\{\hat{\circ}_{i}\})
.
The transport of
\{\circ_{i}\}
by the bijection
\widecheck{(-)}\colon\mathsf{ucpx}\to\mathsf{transv}
, denoted by
\{\check{\circ}_{i}\}
, yields an operad
(\mathsf{transv},\{\check{\circ}_{i}\})
.
The same holds for
\{\circ^{c}_{i}\}
.
Moreover we have
\complement^{\prime}(\mathsf{transv},\{\hat{\circ}_{i}\})=(\mathsf{transv},\{\check{\circ}^{c}_{i}\})
and
\complement^{\prime}(\mathsf{transv},\{\hat{\circ}^{c}_{i}\})=(\mathsf{transv},\{\check{\circ}_{i}\})

Remark 3.9
.

Note that we need to transport
\{\circ_{i}\}
since composing a transversal family to the right with the transversal family
\{\varnothing\}
might not yield a transversal family. For example, we have that
\{\{1,2\},\{2,3\}\}\circ_{3}\{\varnothing_{1}\}=\{\{1,2\},\{2\}\}
is not a transversal family because
\{2\}\subseteq\{1,2\}
, then using either
\{\hat{\circ}_{i}\}
or
\{\check{\circ}_{i}\}
helps us choose either
\{1,2\}
or
\{2\}
, respectively.
However, If we denote by
\mathsf{transv}_{\neq\{\varnothing\}}(n)=\mathsf{transv}(n)\setminus\{\{\varnothing_{n}\}\}
, then
(\mathsf{transv}_{\neq\{\varnothing\}},\{\circ_{i}\})
is an operad, which coincides with the two of
Proposition

3.8
restricted to
\mathsf{transv}_{\neq\varnothing}
. Similarly,
(\mathsf{transv}_{\neq\{[n]\}},\{\circ^{c}_{i}\})
coincides with both
(\mathsf{transv}_{\neq\{[n]\}},\{\hat{\circ}^{c}_{i}\})
and
(\mathsf{transv}_{\neq\{[n]\}},\{\check{\circ}^{c}_{i}\})
.

Note that that it is enough to only consider the facets when computing the compositions
K\circ_{i}L
or
K\circ_{i}^{c}L
, of two non-empty non-trivial simplicial complexes. In particular we have the following.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
K
and
L
be non-empty non-trivial simplicial complexes on
[n]
and
[m]
, respectively, and let
i\in[n]
.
Then, the facets of
K\circ_{i}L
are as follows.

•

F^{<i}\sqcup(F^{\prime}+i-1)\sqcup(F^{>i}+m-1)
, for every facet
F
of
K
containing
i
, and every facet
F^{\prime}
of
L
, and

•

F^{<i}\sqcup(F^{>i}+m-1)
, for every facet
F
of
K
not containing
i
.

The facets of
K\circ_{i}^{c}L
are as follows.

•

F^{<i}\sqcup([m]+i-1)\sqcup(F^{>i}+m-1)
, for every facet
F
of
K
containing
i
, and

•

F^{<i}\sqcup(F^{\prime}+i-1)\sqcup(F^{>i}+m-1)
, for every facet
F
of
K
not containing
i
, and every facet
F^{\prime}
of
L
.

Proof.

This comes from the fact that
K
and
L
are non-empty and non-trivial and a direct inspection of
Equation

3.1
and
Equation

3.2
.
∎

3.4.
Suboperads and their modules on the substitution and composition operads

In addition to the ideal generated by the empty simplicial complexes, which is a suboperad of both
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
and
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
, we have the following three suboperads.
Moreover, their left or right actions onto
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
are well understood.
Recall that the unit of
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
is the simplicial complex
\pm\coloneqq\{\varnothing,\{1\}\}
.
Given a simplicial complex
K
on
[m]
, then an element
i\in[m]
is a
ghost vertex
of
K
if
\{i\}
is not a face of
K
.
Moreover, we denote by
\Delta_{[n]}
the
n
-simplex
, whose facet is
[n]
are all the subsets of
[n]
, and
\partial\Delta_{[n]}
the
boundary of the
n
-simplex
whose facets are the subsets of size
n-1
of
[n]
.

Proposition 3.11
.

The following three
\mathbb{S}
-collections, equipped with the composition
\{\circ_{i}\}
, are suboperads of
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
and
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}\})
:

(1)

\cal T_{n}=\{\{\varnothing\}\}
, for
n\geq 1
, whose only operation with arity
n
is the trivial simplicial complex on
n
vertices (non-unital),

(2)

\cal D_{n}=\{\{\varnothing,\{1\},\ldots,\{n\}\}\}
, for
n\geq 1
, whose only operation with arity
n
is a simplicial complex having
n
discrete points (unital), and

(3)

\cal S_{n}=\{\Delta_{[n]}\}
, for
n\geq 1
, whose only operation with arity
n
is the
n
-simplex (unital).

Furthermore, these operads are isomorphic to
\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
as they have only one operation per arity.

Proof.

The
\mathbb{S}
-collection
(\varnothing_{n})_{n\geq 1}
trivially forms a non-unital suboperad of
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}
, and
\cal T
is its image by the endofunctor
\eta
, which hence yields a (non-unital) suboperad of
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}\})
by
Corollary

2.10
.
Then, we have
\cal D=\eta(\eta(\operatorname{\mathit{Perm}}))^{\downarrow}
.
Finally,
\cal S=\eta(\cal O)^{\downarrow}
for
\cal O
the unital suboperad of
\operatorname{\mathit{IdemCom}}
generated by the
\mathbb{S}
-collection
([n])_{n\geq 1}
.
∎

Recall that the unit of
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
is the simplicial complex
\{\varnothing\}
on the vertex set
[1]
.
Similarly, we have four suboperads of
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
which appear as follows.

Proposition 3.12
.

The following three
\mathbb{S}
-collections, equipped with the composition
\{\circ_{i}^{c}\}
, are suboperads of
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
and
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
:

(1)

\cal T
(unital),

(2)

\cal S_{n}=\{\Delta_{[n]}\}
, for
n\geq 1
(non-unital), and

(3)

\partial\cal S_{n}=\{\partial\Delta_{[n]}\}
, for
n\geq 1
(unital).

Furthermore, these operads are isomorphic to
\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
as they have only one operation per arity.

Proof.

First,
(\cal T,\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
is a suboperad of
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
since the only face of
\{\varnothing\}
is
\varnothing
and contain no
i\in[n]
, for every
n\geq 1
, hence we have
\{\varnothing\}\circ_{i}\{\varnothing\}=\{\varnothing\}
.
The fact that
(\cal S,\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
is a suboperad of
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
comes from
\Delta_{[n]}\circ_{i}\Delta_{[m]}=\Delta_{[n+m-1]}
.
Then, we have
(\partial\cal S,\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})={(\complement^{\prime}(\widehat{\cal D},\{\circ_{i}\}))}^{\downarrow}
, which hence is a suboperad of
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
as
(\cal D,\{\circ_{i}\})
is a suboperad of
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}\})
by
Proposition

3.11
, and since
\complement^{\prime}
transforms
\{\circ_{i}\}
to
\{\circ_{i}^{c}\}
, by
Proposition

3.7
, and together with
Proposition

3.8
and
Remark

3.9
since
(\widehat{\cal D},\{\circ_{i}\})
is a suboperad of
(\mathsf{transv}_{\neq\{\varnothing\}},\{\circ_{i}\})
. ∎

Remark 3.13
.

As all these suboperads
\cal O
are isomorphic to
\operatorname{\mathit{Com}}
and are hence generated by their operations of arity
2
.
Thus, we can completely describe left and right
\cal O
-modules on either
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}\})
,
(\mathsf{scpx},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
,
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}\})
, or
(\mathsf{scpx}_{\neq\varnothing},\{\circ_{i}^{c}\})
, by computing the left or right composition with their operations with arity
2
.
Let
T_{n}
, resp.
D_{n}
, be the unique operation of
\cal T
, resp.
\cal D
with arity
n
.
We describe every left and right modules in
Table

1
, and provide more detailed explanations below.

and
